Aeroacoustic measurements and numerical predictions are commonly used to determine the sound generation from fluid flows. This work documents the measurement of the aerodynamic sound generation by a circular cylinder in crossflow. Three different velocities are studied, and based on the experimental data, a simplified aeroacoustic sound prediction workflow is presented to estimate the radiated far-field noise. The experimental dataset and the numerical simulation setup in open-source software packages are provided online. The geometrical and flow definitions, along with the experimental dataset, constitute a standardised configuration (called https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.13365560) proposed as a validation benchmark for numerical simulation workflows and as a reference for the design of measurement facilities. A strongly simplified 2D numerical simulation using OpenFoam and openCFS is provided with the emphasis to run on a typical personal computer hardware. As a consequence, the provided numerical simulation of the flow is not resolving the turbulent structures nor the 3D characteristics of the flow which are known from experimental and numerical studies.

**Use in this project:** Examined the dataset description, simulation workflow, validation discussion and limitations. FLOW-01 provides experimental cylinder-flow data plus a simplified open-source two-dimensional workflow intended to run on ordinary computers. It explicitly acknowledges missing three-dimensional turbulent structure and corrections used for comparison. The transferable contribution is a standardized case with usable metadata and honest fidelity limits. Your existing recordings cannot serve as its equivalent ground-truth localization dataset.

Task-level decoding accuracy and perceptual fidelity also ranked the auditory representations differently: ANF-based decoding achieved higher physical localization accuracy under some acoustic conditions, whereas the MSO-based pathway better reproduced characteristic human lateral-error patterns. The results show that flexible neural decoders can reproduce key features of human spatial perception while maintaining a link between auditory coding and behavior.

Rotating sound source localization is commonly performed using rotating beamforming techniques, such as Mode Composition Beamforming (MCB), which require a prescribed rotational speed in post-processing. In practice, the mean rotational speed prescribed in post-processing may differ from the reference window-mean speed, yet the impact of this prescribed mean-speed bias on rotating beamforming results is not described by a unified, dimensionless metric. This study introduces the Rotational Mismatch Index (RMI), a dimensionless quantity that condenses frequency, radius of rotation, analysis window length, and rotational speed bias into a single indicator. For RMI below approximately 0.5, the degradation is found to be practically negligible, whereas the range 0.5 ≤ RMI < 1 corresponds to clearly visible but moderate effects, and RMI values above unity lead to distinctly degraded localization. The proposed RMI-based criteria offer practical tolerance guidelines for the design and quality control of rotating beamforming measurements.

**Use in this project:** Examined the mismatch derivation, tolerance formulas, validation discussion and conclusions. Derives a dimensionless phase-mismatch index coupling frequency, radius, window length and rotational-speed bias; tests numerical trends and a public fan dataset. Explicitly limits the index's universality and separates primary scaling from secondary influences. This is a good model for turning your error analysis into a bounded practical rule, but its rotating-source formulas and empirical thresholds do not transfer to stationary three-microphone GCC-PHAT. Your native-samples-per-baseline heuristic needs its own derivation and validation.

## Forthcoming and unresolved records — separate from the published count

### 2026-07-09 — The start of acoustic research at Chalmers University of Technology

Wolfgang Kropp; Krister Larsson; Pontus Thorsson

[10.1051/aacus/2026067](https://doi.org/10.1051/aacus/2026067) · Volume , article 

**Screening:** Title and abstract screened.

**Topics:** Other acoustics.

At the beginning of the 20th century building acoustics developed from a more empirical field to a fully developed sub-discipline in acoustics. This was supported by the development of measurement techniques required for carrying out laboratory and field measurements and by people engaged in building up acoustic laboratories. So also happened in Sweden in the middle of the forties when Per Brüel came to Chalmers University of Technology where he started the Chalmers Acoustic Laboratory. He was soon joined by Uno Ingard who later was professor at the MIT. At that time Sweden was in a transformation process from an agrarian to a highly industrialized nation leading to a large urbanization. Shortage of housing and the need to increase housing standard led to the so-called million programme with the goal to build one million flats. New building technologies led however to buildings of varying acoustic quality. As consequence building acoustics was identified as an important part in the education of civil engineers. In 1962 Chalmers established a professorship in building acoustics. In 1969 Tor Kihlman became the first professor in building acoustics in Sweden. That was the start of Applied Acoustics at Chalmers University of Technology.

Accurate State of Charge (SoC) estimation is essential for the safe and reliable operation of lithium-ion batteries. Conventional electrical methods often require complex models and are sensitive to temperature variations and hysteresis eects. This work proposes a non-destructive ultrasonic Time-of-Flight (ToF) technique for SoC monitoring. These nding demonstrate the potential of ultrasonic sensing for real-time, in situ SoC estimation and future integration into advanced battery management systems.

Structural health monitoring (SHM) is critical for the safety, reliability, and long-term performance of engineering structures. The findings pave the way for scalable, cost-effective, and computationally efficient solutions to monitor structural integrity in real-time, offering significant benefits for infrastructure management and maintenance.

### 2026-08-11 — Evaluation of Quality of Experience in Dynamic and Static Binaural Rendering with Natural Head Movements

Clément Rappin; Julian Palacino; Pascal Rueff; Laurent Feichter; Mathieu Paquier

[10.1051/aacus/2026086](https://doi.org/10.1051/aacus/2026086) · Volume , article 

**Screening:** Title and abstract screened.

**Topics:** Hearing / spatial perception.

Binaural audio has become one of the most relevant spatialized rendering formats for the general audience. Research has shown that the addition of a head tracking system helps to resolve front/back confusion and improves the sensation of externalization. However, past studies were made in lab conditions using simple sound scenes and imposed head movements. This article investigates the impact of dynamic binaural rendering (over a static binaural rendering) in ecological conditions. The Quality of Experience (QoE), along with several perceptual attributes, was rated in an absolute judgment protocol. Static and dynamic binaural renderings were evaluated in an object-based audio format with 10 excerpts of various audio content (music, radio drama, podcast) produced by professional audio engineers in 3D audio. The subjects could freely move their heads during their listening. The results showed that the ratings in QoE were significantly increased when listening to binaural rendering with a head tracking system. The perceived sensation of externalization and immersion was also improved. However, the investigated perceptual attributes were not predominant in the ratings of QoE, and further investigations on the usefulness of head tracking in different contexts remain to be studied. Past results on the impact of head tracking (like improved externalization) were confirmed with complex scenes and natural head movements.

### 2026-08-12 — Simultaneous acousto-optic and vibration reconstruction with optical interferometry

Tianxing Zhong; Samuel Arturo Verburg; Efren Fernandez-Grande; Jonas Brunskog

[10.1051/aacus/2026085](https://doi.org/10.1051/aacus/2026085) · Volume , article 

**Screening:** Title and abstract screened.

**Topics:** Other acoustics.

Acousto-optic sensing is an acoustic measurement technique based on the interaction effect between sound and light, generally using optical interferometry. However, acousto-optic measurements from an interferometer can be biased by mechanical vibrations of the laser back-scattering surface. In this paper, a combined model that includes acoustic plane wave and vibration eigenmode basis functions is proposed to describe both the acousto-optic effect and vibrations of the back-scattering surface. Simulations an experiment demonstrated the viability of separating and simultaneously reconstructing both the acoustic and vibration field, thus enhancing the quality of acousto-optic sound field reconstruction under excessive background vibrations

### 2026-08-12 — Sound absorption characteristics of honeycomb-like structure with honeycomb core direction reconstruction

Yuxin Feng; Pan Zhou; Daocheng Zhou; Yi Han; Jianghui Xin; Liguo Zang

[10.1051/aacus/2026087](https://doi.org/10.1051/aacus/2026087) · Volume , article 

**Screening:** Title and abstract screened.

**Topics:** Other acoustics.

To address the single shape feature and poor low- to mid-frequency sound absorption of traditional honeycomb structures, this paper proposes a honeycomb-like structure design with reconstructed core direction and diversified geometric configurations. A theoretical model was established and validated by experimental and simulation data. Using the finite element method and control variable approach, a multi-parameter analysis was conducted to investigate the effects of micro-pore radius, micro-perforated plate thickness, number of micro-pores, vertical cavity depth, and horizontal cavity width on the sound absorption coefficient curve. The sound absorption performance of coupling and single structures was compared. Results show that the vertical honeycomb-like structure's peak absorption frequency shifts downward by 120 Hz compared to traditional ones. Key parameters exert quantifiable effects. The 20 mm thick differentially coupling structure achieves an effective absorption bandwidth over 200 Hz in 600-1100 Hz, with more absorption peaks formed by greater parametric differences, providing a new technical solution for noise control in rail transit and industrial equipment.

### 2026-08-25 — Irrelevant speech sound level effects on sustained attention in an open-plan office

Yan Sheng; Ruining Zhang; Yao Fu; Rumei Han; Yuan Zhang

[10.1051/aacus/2026088](https://doi.org/10.1051/aacus/2026088) · Volume , article 

**Screening:** Title and abstract screened.

**Topics:** Other acoustics.

Open-plan offices are valued for flexibility and space efficiency, yet irrelevant speech remains a primary source of acoustic dissatisfaction. This study examined how irrelevant-speech sound pressure level (SPL) affects sustained attention and physiological load in a simulated open-plan office. Twentyfour participants performed the Sustained Attention to Response Task (SART) under four conditions: a background-only control (LAeq 35–40 dB(A)) and irrelevant speech at 40–45, 45–50, and 50–55 dB(A). Behavioural outcomes were analysed alongside electrodermal activity (EDA, n = 24) and prefrontal EEG measures (valid n = 16). Linear mixed-effects models revealed no significant effect of SPL on mean behavioural performance. However, sympathetic arousal differed across conditions: relative change in skin conductance level (∆SCL) was higher at 50–55 dB(A) than at 40–45 dB(A) (p = 0.014, Cohen’s dz = 0.54). In the EEG subset, mean task engagement did not differ between SPLs, but increased over time only at 50–55 dB(A) (p = 0.002). These results indicate that elevated speech levels impose measurable physiological costs even when short-term behavioural performance is maintained, supporting the integration of physiological indicators into post-occupancy evaluation and the prioritisation of design measures to limit speech levels in open-plan workplaces.

### 2026-08-25 — From bellows to pipe foot: experimental and lumped-parameter analysis of steady-state and transient pressure in a flue organ wind system

Gonzalo Villegas Curulla; Augustin Ernoult; Benjamin Véron; Benoît Fabre; Brian F. G. Katz

[10.1051/aacus/2026089](https://doi.org/10.1051/aacus/2026089) · Volume , article 

**Screening:** Title and abstract screened.

**Topics:** Other acoustics.

The sound produced by an organ pipe results from a strong coupling between the pipe and its wind supply system, which governs both steady-state characteristics and transient behaviour. While detailed models of organ wind systems exist, their complexity and large number of parameters hinder their practical use and obscure the link between these parameters and the observed behaviours. This study investigates to what extent a simplified one-dimensional model can reproduce and explain the evolution of supply pressure in a real pipe organ, and how far such a model can be reduced while preserving the essential dynamics. Pressure measurements were carried out at several locations in an organ wind system, using a robotic key-actuation device to maximise repeatability. These measurements were compared with simulations of a reduced lumped-parameter model based on interconnected cavities and channels. A ``three cavity -- three channel'' formulation is proposed, characterised by a small set of physically interpretable time constants. The model successfully reproduces the measured steady pressures and transient rising times for all pipes, highlighting its ability to interpret experimental data and make the link between model parameters and observed behaviour. Some limitations are discussed: the assumption that the pressure is homogeneous in cavities and the lack of link between effective and geometric parameters.

### 2026-09-07 — Assessment of vibration transmission reduction through plate junctions with metamaterial treatment

Sofie Becuwe; Stijn Moons; Daniele Giannini; Edwin P.B. Reynders

[10.1051/aacus/2026090](https://doi.org/10.1051/aacus/2026090) · Volume , article 

**Screening:** Title and abstract screened.

**Topics:** Other acoustics.

Reducing vibration transmission through plate junctions is challenging, as conventional strategies, including structural decoupling or addition of mass, often increase structural complexity, cost, and material use, while remaining ineffective at low frequencies. This study explores resonant metamaterial treatments as a lightweight alternative, in which a strip of subwavelength resonators is applied along the junction to achieve targeted vibration transmission reduction. As a starting point, this work focuses on junctions between thin isotropic plates. To predict the vibration transmission through plate junctions treated with resonators, a two-step modelling approach is developed. First, all transmission coefficients across the plate junction are predicted using an analytical wave-based formulation, in which the plates are assumed to be semi-infinite and the metamaterial treatment is incorporated through a frequency dependent mass density. Second, these transmission coefficients serve as input to a statistical energy analysis framework to predict the velocity level difference between a source and receiver plate. The model is subsequently used to explore the influence of resonator mass, resonator strip width and multi-modal resonance behaviour on the vibration attenuation performance. Finally, an experimental validation is conducted by considering two aluminum plates separated by a junction treated with a strip of resonators. The plates are excited using an electrodynamic shaker, and their out‑of‑plane vibration field is measured using scanning laser Doppler vibrometry. The results confirm clear vibration attenuation at the target frequency and good agreement between predicted and measured velocity level differences.
